\par\endthebibliography\par\lx@newpage\par\par\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{appendix}{Appendix Contents}\immediate\lx@newpage\par\par\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{Notation Glossary}
\par\begin{table}\centering\begin{tabular}[]{@{}lp{12cm}@{}}\hline\cr\hline\cr{Symbol}&Meaning\\
\hline\cr\lx@intercol{Alphabets and Strings}\hfil\lx@intercol \\
\hline\cr${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace$&Symbol alphabet (characters or tokens).\\
${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace$&Output alphabet of the transducer.\\
${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Xi}\xspace$&Finite alphabet over which units are strings; ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Xi}\xspace^{*}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace={\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Xi}\xspace\sqcup\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace\}$ (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:regularity}).\\
${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}$&Unit inventory chosen by the modeler (countable, possibly infinite).\\
${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$&Distinguished separator symbol marking unit boundaries; ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace\notin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Xi}\xspace$.\\
${{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textsc{eos}}}$&End-of-sequence symbol; ${{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textsc{eos}}}\notin\Sigma$.\\
$\Sigma^{*}$&Kleene closure: set of all finite strings over alphabet $\Sigma$, including $\varepsilon$.\\
$\Sigma^{+}$&Non-empty strings: $\Sigma^{*}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}$.\\
\hline\cr\lx@intercol{Units and Contexts}\hfil\lx@intercol \\
\hline\cr$T$&Length of a string or utterance (number of symbols or units).\\
${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{t}$&The $t$-th unit in an utterance; ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{t}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}$.\\
${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace$&Utterance: sequence of units, ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{1}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{T}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}^{*}$.\\
${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{<t}$&Preceding-unit context.\\
${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace_{[i,j)}$&Region of interest (ROI): subspan ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{i}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{j-1}$.\\
\hline\cr\lx@intercol{Maps and Transducers}\hfil\lx@intercol \\
\hline\cr${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\rho}\xspace$&Unit parser (stochastic map ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\rho}\xspace\colon{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace^{*}\rightsquigarrow{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}^{*}$); assumed deterministic (${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace^{*}\to{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}^{*}$) in this paper.\\
${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\rho}^{-1}\xspace$&Realization: a relation ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\rho}^{-1}\xspace\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}^{*}\times{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace^{*}$ mapping unit strings to symbol strings.\\
${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}f}\xspace$&String-to-string relation ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}f}\xspace\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace^{*}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace^{*}$.\\
${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}$&Finite transducer with input alphabet ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace$ and output alphabet ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace$.\\
${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}D}$&Set of delimiter symbols; ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}D}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace$.\\
${{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{L}},\;{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{T}}$&Acontextual (delimiter-based) FSTs: leading and trailing ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ insertion.\\
${{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{ptb}}$&Contextual FST implementing Penn Treebank segmentation rules.\\
${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace$&Homomorphism ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Xi}\xspace^{*}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ appending ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ to each unit's underlying string; extended to ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}^{*}$ by concatenation (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:unit-homomorphism}).\\
${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}h}^{-1}\xspace$&Inverse of ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace$: splits on ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ and maps each segment to its unit (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:transduced-lms}).\\
\hline\cr\lx@intercol{Probability and Surprisal}\hfil\lx@intercol \\
\hline\cr${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace}}$&Source/token-level language model (probability distribution) over ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace^{*}$.\\
${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace}}$&Transduced language model over ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace^{*}$, defined as ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}$.\\
${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}}}$&Unit-level language model over ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}^{*}$.\\
${{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{\mathrm{H}}}}$&Implicit human language model assumed by surprisal theory.\\
${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}s}$&Surprisal of a unit in context: $-\log{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{t}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\boldsymbol{u}}}_{<t})$.\\
\hline\cr\lx@intercol{Reading-time Analysis}\hfil\lx@intercol \\
\hline\cr${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r}_{\pi}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}^{n}_{t},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\boldsymbol{u}}}^{n}_{<t})$&Reading-time measurement (first-fixation, gaze, or total) for unit ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}^{n}_{t}$ from participant $\pi$.\\
${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{x}}^{n}_{t}$&Predictor vector $(x_{1,t}^{n},\ldots,x_{J,t}^{n})^{\top}$ at position $t$ of utterance $n$.\\
$N,\;n$&Number of training utterances; index over training utterances.\\
$M,\;m$&Number of held-out (test) utterances; index over test utterances.\\
$\text{LL}_{\text{bl}},\;\text{LL}_{\text{tgt}}$&Mean per-observation held-out log-likelihood of baseline / target GAMM.\\
$\Delta_{\text{llh}}$&Improvement in held-out log-likelihood: $\text{LL}_{\text{tgt}}-\text{LL}_{\text{bl}}$.\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{tabular}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{1}}{Notation used throughout the paper.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Table 1}}{Notation used throughout the paper.}}}
\@add@centering\end{table}\lx@newpage\par\par\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{Prefix-Freeness of $\transH$}
\par In \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:unit-homomorphism} we place ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ at the \emph{right} edge of each unit, i.e., ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ marks a unit's completion, so that ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\boldsymbol{\xi}}\xspace_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$. One could equivalently consider the mirror convention
\begin{equation*}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace_{\mathrm{l}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}})\mathrel{\overset{\raisebox{-0.75346pt}{{def}}}{=}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\boldsymbol{\xi}}\xspace_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}},\end{equation*}in which ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ instead marks a unit's \emph{onset}. Both are monoid homomorphisms ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}^{*}\to{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace^{*}$ whose images ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U})$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace_{\mathrm{l}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U})$ are regular and related by a 1-symbol shift of ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$. The pushforward identity \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:next-unit}, however, is an equality only under the completion convention. The reason, as we show below, is that the completion convention makes ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U})$ a prefix-free code, whereas the onset convention does not.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Reduction.} Using ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace{\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace){\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}})$, \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:next-unit} reduces to the prefix identity
\begin{equation}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\rho}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\xspace)\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\boldsymbol{u}}}\iff{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\rho}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\xspace))\succeq{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\boldsymbol{u}}}),\end{equation}for all ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\xspace\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace^{*}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\boldsymbol{u}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}^{*}$.
The $(\Rightarrow)$ direction holds for any monoid homomorphism; only the $(\Leftarrow)$ direction depends on the placement of ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Completion ($\SEP$ trailing).} Writing ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\boldsymbol{u}}}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{1}{\cdot}\cdots{\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{k}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\rho}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\xspace)={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}^{\prime}_{1}{\cdot}\cdots{\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}^{\prime}_{T}$, we have
\@@amsalign
Since ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace\notin{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Xi}\xspace$, every ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ in ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\rho}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\xspace))$ sits at a block boundary. Hence, if ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\rho}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\xspace))\succeq{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\boldsymbol{u}}})$, then the \emph{final} ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ of ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\boldsymbol{u}}})$ must coincide with the block boundary after ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\boldsymbol{\xi}}\xspace_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}^{\prime}_{k}}$. Matching back block-by-block forces ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}^{\prime}_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{i}$ for $i\leq k$, and thus ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\rho}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\boldsymbol{\sigma}}\xspace)\succeq{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\boldsymbol{u}}}$.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Onset ($\SEP$ leading).} Under ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace_{\mathrm{l}}$, the image ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace_{\mathrm{l}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\boldsymbol{u}}})$ ends with the bare bytes ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\boldsymbol{\xi}}\xspace_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{k}}$: the ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ that would close ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{k}$ is absorbed into the onset of ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{k+1}$ and is therefore absent from ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace_{\mathrm{l}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{\boldsymbol{u}}})$. A byte-prefix match then admits any ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}$ whose underlying string begins with ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\boldsymbol{\xi}}\xspace_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{k}}$, not only ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{k}$ itself.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Counter-example.} Let ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace=\{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}},{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{b}}\}$, ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Xi}\xspace=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{a}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{b}}\}$, and ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace={\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Xi}\xspace\sqcup\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace\}$. Take ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{a},{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{ab}\}\subset{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Xi}\xspace^{*}$ with underlying strings ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\boldsymbol{\xi}}\xspace_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{a}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{a}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\boldsymbol{\xi}}\xspace_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{ab}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{ab}}$, a deterministic ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\rho}\xspace$ satisfying ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\rho}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}})={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{a}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\rho}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{ab}})={\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{ab}$, and source distribution ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{a}})={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace}}({\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{ab}})=\tfrac{1}{2}$, so that ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{a})=\tfrac{1}{2}$. Under the onset convention, the transducer produces target strings ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{a}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{ab}}$, both of which have ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace_{\mathrm{l}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{a})={\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{a}}$ as a byte prefix; scoring with the transduced LM therefore gives ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace_{\mathrm{l}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{a}))=1\neq\tfrac{1}{2}$. Under the completion convention, ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{a}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ is not a byte prefix of ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{ab}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$, and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{a}}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace)=\tfrac{1}{2}$ matches ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{a})$ exactly.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Practical consequence.} The trailing ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ inside each ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{k})$ pins the unit's right boundary in the target-byte prefix to a block boundary of the parse, ruling out parses in which ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}_{k}$ has been silently extended into a longer unit sharing its byte prefix. Whitespace-delimited English inventories contain many such byte-prefix overlaps---{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{the}}$\subset${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{there}}, {\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{in}}$\subset${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{into}}, {\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{a}}$\subset${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{an}}$\subset${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{and}}---so the onset convention would leak probability mass pervasively in practice.
\lx@newpage\par\par\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{Transducers}
\par Here we provide additional details on the FSTs used in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:experiments}. We implement all FSTs in Pynini \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{gorman-2016-pynini}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, a Python library for compiling and composing finite transducers that builds on OpenFST \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{riley-etal-2009-openfst}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Characters}
As shown by \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{snbjarnarson2026transducing}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} (see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:subwordST}), the transformation from tokens to characters can be encoded using an FST. In this work, we do not compile the FST for the experiments in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{app:experiments}; instead, we use the algorithms and implementations of \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{pmlr-v267-vieira25a}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} to transform ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace}}$ into a character-level model.

\par\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{6}}{A finite transducer for mapping a token-level LM to characters, illustrated with paths for {\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0} \textvisiblespace}{}cat}} and {\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{Dog}}. Adapted from \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{snbjarnarson2026transducing}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Figure 6}}{A finite transducer for mapping a token-level LM to characters, illustrated with paths for {\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0} \textvisiblespace}{}cat}} and {\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{Dog}}. Adapted from \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{snbjarnarson2026transducing}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}.}}}
\@add@centering\@add@centering\end{figure}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Acontextual Words}
Recent work argues that the common leading-whitespace convention of many BPE tokenizers---where the space preceding a word is bundled with the word's first token---induces a misallocation of surprisal, and that probability mass should instead be attributed as trailing whitespace, i.e., assigned to the preceding word \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{oh-schuler-2024-leading, pimentel-meister-2024-compute}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}. At the same time, \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{giulianelli-etal-2024-proper}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} argue that there is no universally correct convention and the attribution of whitespace should be chosen to match the experimental setup.
Note that under the definition of \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{oh-schuler-2024-leading}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} and \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{pimentel-meister-2024-compute}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}, leading-versus-trailing whitespace decoding can be interpreted as an aggregation method that specifies how probability mass is redistributed across unit boundaries. Here, we express leading and trailing attribution as two finite transducers, ${{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{L}}$ and ${{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{T}}$; see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:delimiter_transducers}. This leading-versus-trailing choice concerns the \emph{delimiter byte's} unit membership (i.e., which unit the space belongs to) and is a separate axis from the trailing-${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ convention on ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace$ itself; see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{app:trailing-h} for why the latter must be trailing regardless. In our experiments, we set the delimiter set to ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}D}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{\textvisiblespace}}\}$, so whitespace is the sole signal of a unit boundary.
\par Note that a third variant, in which whitespace is absorbed into the separator ($q_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{d}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace}q_{0}$) so that no unit ever contains a whitespace character, is easy to construct by replacing the delimiter arcs in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:delimiter_transducers}.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Contextual Words}
We represent contextual words using the rules described in the Penn Treebank annotation guidelines and encode each rule as a small context-dependent string-rewrite transducer (see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:contextual_rule_main} for an example of such a rule), using Pynini\textquoteright s rewrite calculus (e.g., replace operations with explicit left/right contexts and boundary conditions). The full tokenizer is obtained by composing these rule transducers left-to-right, yielding a single transducer that maps input strings to their PTB-style tokenized form.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Transducer Sizes}
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:transducer-sizes} reports the number of states and arcs of each FST ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}$ used in the experiments, together with its number of \emph{universal} states, i.e., those states, where the corresponding input-projected FSA accepts every symbol ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\sigma}\in{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace$ (see \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{snbjarnarson2026transducing}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} for a detailed discussion). Universal states can be handled more efficiently in the algorithms provided by \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{snbjarnarson2026transducing}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}, so transducers with a larger universal fraction yield a higher throughput (Syms/s).
\par\begin{table*}\centering\begin{minipage}[165.59853pt]\centering\begin{tabular}[]{@{}lrrr@{}}\hline\cr\hline\cr Transducer&States&Arcs&Universal\\
\hline\cr Acontextual (leading)&3&517&3 (all)\\
Acontextual (trailing)&258&1{,}029&258 (all)\\
Contextual&361&35{,}210&78 (partial)\\

\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{2}}{Size of each finite transducer ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}$ used in the experiments, together with the number of universal states \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{snbjarnarson2026transducing}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}; ``all'' indicates that every state is universal.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Table 2}}{Size of each finite transducer ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}$ used in the experiments, together with the number of universal states \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{snbjarnarson2026transducing}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}; ``all'' indicates that every state is universal.}}}

\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{3}}{{Overlap with whitespace stripped}: percentage of units in the row inventory whose form---after removing any attributed whitespace---is also a unit in the column inventory. The acontextual leading and trailing variants yield identical stripped forms and are merged here.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Table 3}}{{Overlap with whitespace stripped}: percentage of units in the row inventory whose form---after removing any attributed whitespace---is also a unit in the column inventory. The acontextual leading and trailing variants yield identical stripped forms and are merged here.}}}
\@add@centering\end{minipage}\@add@centering\end{table*}\par The two delimiter-insertion transducers ${{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{L}}$ and ${{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{T}}$ exhibit a size asymmetry despite being drawn as equivalent three-state machines in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:delimiter_transducers}. This is because the compiled FSTs allow only one output symbol per arc, while the figure uses a shorthand to draw arcs with multiple-symbol outputs. Each such arc is compiled into a chain of two arcs through an auxiliary state, with one auxiliary per input-byte value. In ${{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{L}}$ the multi-symbol output sits on the delimiter arc $q_{1}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{d}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{d}}}q_{0}$, so only $|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}D}|$ auxiliary states are introduced; with ${\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}D}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{\textvisiblespace}}\}$ the single resulting auxiliary is merged away by minimization, leaving three states. In ${{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}}_{\mathrm{T}}$ the multi-symbol output sits on the arc $q_{0}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\texttt{x}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{x}}}q_{1}$, so $|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace\setminus{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}D}|$ auxiliaries are introduced; with a byte alphabet ($|{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace|=256$) that is $255$ auxiliaries whose distinct output labels prevent merging, giving $3+255=258$ states.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{Dataset Details}
We reprocess the English portion of the MECO eye-tracking corpus \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{siegelman2022expanding}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, which contains scanpaths from 46 readers recorded while reading 12 short Wikipedia excerpts. \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:units_stats} reports the number of observations at each stage of the analysis pipeline, from raw units to the final GAMM input, for each of the unit inventories.

\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{5}}{{Overlap with whitespace kept} (the unit text used by our GAMMs): percentage of units in the row inventory that appear \emph{verbatim}, including any attributed whitespace, as units in the column inventory.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Table 5}}{{Overlap with whitespace kept} (the unit text used by our GAMMs): percentage of units in the row inventory that appear \emph{verbatim}, including any attributed whitespace, as units in the column inventory.}}}
\@add@centering\end{table}\par With whitespace stripped (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:unit-overlap-stripped}), the three word-like inventories share 71--88\% of their units pairwise. With whitespace kept (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:unit-overlap-ws}), the two acontextual variants become nearly disjoint, and the contextual inventory, whose units never contain whitespace, is nearly disjoint from all three. GPT-2 tokens, which use leading-space by convention, overlap tightly with acontextual leading but not with acontextual trailing.
\par Looking at the whitespace-stripped differences: the 604 GPT-2 occurrences absent from the acontextual inventory are mainly punctuation marks that BPE splits off (126 commas, 96 periods) and BPE subword fragments (e.g., {\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{Jan}}, {\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}{us}} from {Janus}; conversely, the 325 acontextual occurrences absent from GPT-2 are words that BPE splits into multiple tokens (e.g., {Janus}, {thylacine}, {performance-enhancing}). Acontextual and contextual words differ mainly on punctuation attachment: the contextual inventory splits off 129 commas and 11 periods that the acontextual inventory attaches to the preceding word (e.g., acontextual {\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{organizations,}} vs.\ contextual {\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{organizations}}\,$|$\,{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{,}}); possessives ({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{'s}}) and quotation marks ({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{``}}, {\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{''}}) account for the remaining contextual-only units.\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Unit and Fixation Visualizations}
\lx@cref{creftypepluralcap\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:viz-model-tokens}, \lx@cref{refnum}{fig:viz-ws-lead}, \lx@cref{refnum}{fig:viz-ws-trail}, \lx@cref{refnum}{fig:viz-ptb} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{fig:viz-character} show the same trial (Reader~3, Text~1 from the MECO English corpus) segmented under the unit inventories used in our experiments, together with the recorded fixation data. Coloured backgrounds mark unit boundaries, and each fixation is coloured by the unit it is attributed to. In the contextual (PTB) inventory, a sentence-final period is split off as its own unit typically only when it is followed by ${{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\textsc{eos}}}$; periods that end a sentence mid-trial remain attached to the preceding word.\par\begin{figure*}\centering\includegraphics[width]{images/trials/model_tokens_leading_reader_03_text_01.pdf}

\@add@centering\end{figure*}\lx@newpage\par\par\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{Experimental Details}
To compute surprisal estimates for the experiments in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:experiments}, we use the implementation by \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{snbjarnarson2026transducing}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} to compose GPT-2 Small \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{radford2019language}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} from the \raisebox{-0.86108pt}{\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{\includegraphics[height]{emoji/huggingface-LaTeX}}}\kern 3.0ptHugging Face hub~\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{wolf-etal-2020-transformers}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} with the respective transducers described in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:units}. To convert token-level models to character-level, we use \raisebox{-1.72218pt}{\includegraphics[height]{emoji/GenLMBytes}}. To quickly compute next-token/byte distributions, we use {\raisebox{-0.86108pt}{\includegraphics[height]{emoji/vllm}}} \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{kwon-etal-2023-vllm}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}.\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Computing Surprisal}
\par Both contextual surprisal and unigram surprisal are computed under the transduced language model ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}$, from the next-unit conditional distribution ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}})\mid{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace))$ of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:next-unit}. Following ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace$ as defined in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:unit-homomorphism}, each unit's byte extension ends with a ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ that marks its right boundary. Per-unit conditional probability is therefore the ratio
\begin{equation}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace_{<t})\;=\;\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace}}}}\!\big({\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace_{<t}){\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}})\big)}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace}}}}\!\big({\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace_{<t})\big)},\end{equation}in which the numerator is a byte-level prefix mass closed off by the ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ carried in ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}})$, making ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace$ prefix-free (see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fn:prefix-free}). The two quantities (contextual vs.\ unigram surprisal) differ only in how they consume this conditional: contextual surprisal scores the unit that actually occurred at each position, whereas unigram surprisal computes the marginal ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}})=\mathbb{E}_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace_{<t}}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace_{<t})]$ with respect to contexts ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace_{<t}$ sampled from the LM, with ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}$ held fixed. Both share the hyperparameters listed in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:surprisal-params}.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Contextual surprisal.}
For each of the 12 MECO trials we first apply the transducer ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}$ at the trial level to obtain the transduced string ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace^{*}$, then score its symbols left-to-right. Each step issues one call to the fast next-symbol decomposition (\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}decompose\_next}}}) algorithm of \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{snbjarnarson2026transducing}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{, \lx@sectionsign C.4)}}}, which returns the full next-symbol distribution over ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace$. Consecutive calls are cached, so advancing the context from ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{<t}$ to ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{<t+1}$ extends the cached decomposition by a single symbol rather than recomputing from scratch; low-probability beams are pruned during expansion using the thresholds in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:surprisal-params}.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Unigram surprisal.}
Unigram surprisal can be estimated using the same LM that estimates the surprisal of ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}$ under ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}}}$ by marginalizing over contexts \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{hopton2026unigram}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}. Because the full marginalization is intractable, we compute a Monte Carlo estimate by drawing $S$ samples from the LM, and for each sample, average the next-unit conditional ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace_{<t})$ over every unit position $t$. For the {GPT-2 token} and {character} inventories, the unit alphabet coincides with the native output alphabet of the LM, so the conditional ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace_{<t})$ is read off directly from the LM. For {acontextual} and {contextual} inventories, units are defined through ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}$ and ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace_{<t})$ must be recovered from ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace}}}}$: We first transduce each sample from ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace}}$ to its target string ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{s}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace^{*}$ and locate the ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ positions $b_{1}<\cdots<b_{K_{s}}$ that mark unit boundaries. At each boundary $b_{k}$, we cache the closed prefix mass $z=\log{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{s}_{\leq b_{k}})$, i.e., the prefix mass through and including the ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$ at position $b_{k}$, and score every candidate unit ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}\in{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}$ by evaluating $z_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}=\log{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\boldsymbol{\delta}}^{s}_{\leq b_{k}}{\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}h}\xspace({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}))$, so that the next-unit conditional is ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}\boldsymbol{u}}\xspace_{<t})=\exp(z_{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}{u}}-z)$ exactly, following \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:next-unit}.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Efficient scoring for the transduced LM.}
Rather than computing the full next-distribution (\mbox{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}decompose\_next}}}), we use the single-symbol scoring routine of \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{snbjarnarson2026transducing}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} (introduced there for cross-entropy evaluation), which decomposes only ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\delta}$ for a specified target symbol and returns $\log{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace}}}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\boldsymbol{\delta}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\delta})$ directly, skipping the full-vocabulary expansion and the final normalization. We apply this routine one target symbol at a time along the byte extension of each unit, accumulating log prefix masses and recovering the unit's conditional prefix probability by subtracting the cached boundary probability mass. Since all $|{\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}|$ unit extensions at a given boundary begin from the same prefix, their decomposition is shared across units; units with common byte prefixes additionally reuse cached partial extensions.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Parameters.}
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:surprisal-params} lists the hyperparameters for both computations. The beam-search and transduced-LM parameters are shared; the sampling block applies only to unigram estimation.
\par\begin{table}\centering\begin{tabular}[]{@{}lll@{}}\hline\cr\hline\cr{Parameter}&{Value}&{Role}\\
\hline\cr\lx@intercol\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{\includegraphics[height]{emoji/GenLMBytes}} \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{pmlr-v267-vieira25a}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}\hfil\lx@intercol \\
\hline\cr Beam size ($K$)&$5$&Maximum beam width; keeps the $K$ highest-probability beams.\\
Beam prune threshold&$0.001$&Drop beams whose probability mass falls below this threshold.\\
\hline\cr\lx@intercol Transduced-LM inference \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{snbjarnarson2026transducing}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}\hfil\lx@intercol \\
\hline\cr${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\texttt{f}}$ prune threshold ($\tau$)&$0.005$&Drop FST paths with probability mass below~$\tau$.\\
Max expand steps&$5$&Halt expansion of non-universal states after this many steps.\\
Expand stop mass&$0.01$&Halt expansion once the relative remaining probability mass falls below this value.\\
\hline\cr\lx@intercol Sampling for estimating unigram surprisal\hfil\lx@intercol \\
\hline\cr LM ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace}}$&GPT-2 Small&Source language model used in all experiments.\\
\# samples $S$&$500$&Number of samples drawn from ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace}}$ in the unigram estimator.\\
Max length&$50$&Maximum number of tokens per sample.\\
Batch size&$64$&Batch size used when sampling from ${\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{{\color[rgb]{0.5508,0.0391,0.3477}\Sigma}\xspace}}$.\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{tabular}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{6}}{Hyperparameters used to estimate contextual and unigram surprisal. The beam-search parameters apply to both estimators; the sampling parameters apply only to the unigram estimator.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Table 6}}{Hyperparameters used to estimate contextual and unigram surprisal. The beam-search parameters apply to both estimators; the sampling parameters apply only to the unigram estimator.}}}
\@add@centering\end{table}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{GPU Usage \& Runtime}
All experiments were run on NVIDIA GeForce RTX 4090 GPUs and RTX 3090 GPUs, each with 24\,GB of GPU memory. \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:throughput-surprisal} reports scoring throughput for contextual surprisal. The acontextual FSTs process approximately 200 target symbols per second; at this rate, scoring the 12 MECO English trials takes approximately one minute on a single GPU. The contextual FST is more than an order of magnitude slower, due to its large number of states and arcs (see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:transducer-sizes}) and the fact that many of its states are not universal, requiring additional computation to traverse the FST until hitting a universal state; see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{app:transducer-sizes} for a brief discussion and \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{snbjarnarson2026transducing}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} for a detailed discussion on universality.
\par\begin{table}\centering\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{7}}{Contextual surprisal scoring throughput per transducer on GPT-2 Small, aggregated over the 12 MECO English trials. ``Symbols'' is the total number of ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace$-symbols scored across all trials; ``Syms/s'' is the corresponding throughput. The character-level model is omitted because character surprisal is obtained via \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{pmlr-v267-vieira25a}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} rather than through an FST composition.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[: ]{{Table 7}}{Contextual surprisal scoring throughput per transducer on GPT-2 Small, aggregated over the 12 MECO English trials. ``Symbols'' is the total number of ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\Delta}\xspace$-symbols scored across all trials; ``Syms/s'' is the corresponding throughput. The character-level model is omitted because character surprisal is obtained via \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{pmlr-v267-vieira25a}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} rather than through an FST composition.}}}
\begin{tabular}[]{lrrr}\hline\cr\hline\cr Transducer&Symbols&Time (s)&Syms/s\\
\hline\cr Acontextual (leading)&15{,}309&72.0&212.8\\
Acontextual (trailing)&15{,}309&75.2&203.6\\
Contextual&13{,}407&1117.7&12.0\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\end{tabular}\@add@centering\end{table}\par Unigram estimation is considerably more expensive than contextual surprisal, because every ${\color[rgb]{0,0.3516,0.5508}\textsc{sep}}\xspace$-boundary of every sample requires an extension of the cached prefix mass for every unit in ${\color[rgb]{0.3828,0.4492,0.0742}U}$. On GPT-2, typical per-sample scoring of a 211-byte sample takes on the order of $20$~seconds for acontextual (leading), $60$~seconds for acontextual (trailing), and $8$~minutes for the contextual transducer; the relative ordering mirrors \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:throughput-surprisal} because the same FST-decomposition step is the bottleneck in both pipelines. Run sequentially, at 500 samples per unit inventory, this translates to roughly 3 hours (acontextual leading), 8 hours (acontextual trailing), and 2--3 days (contextual) of sequential single-GPU compute, so unigram estimation in practice requires parallelization. Since the outer sample loop runs independently across $s$, we chunk the 500-sample runs into independent jobs. Some chunks hit the per-job wall-clock limit before completing, so the final number of successfully scored samples per inventory is 498 (characters and GPT-2 tokens), 496 (acontextual leading), 497 (acontextual trailing), and 470 (contextual); at these sample sizes, the per-unit probabilities are close to converged, and additional samples shift the estimates only marginally.
\par\lx@newpage\par\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{GAMM Specification}
We model log reading time as a generalized additive mixed model \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{wood-2017-gam}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} fitted with {bam()} from {mgcv} in R, using fast restricted maximum likelihood ({method="fREML"}, {discrete=TRUE}). Each continuous predictor enters the log-mean as a cubic regression spline with up to six basis functions ({bs="cr"}, {k=6}); the model further includes a random intercept for participant and by-participant random slopes for every continuous predictor.
\par\vskip 10.22217pt\begin{minipage}[165.59853pt]\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Baseline model ($\widetilde{\varphi}$).}
\begin{verbatim}
log(rt) ~ s(length, bs="cr", k=6)
+ s(length_prev, bs="cr", k=6)
+ s(length_prev2, bs="cr", k=6)
+ s(unigram_surprisal, bs="cr", k=6)
+ s(unigram_surprisal_prev, bs="cr", k=6)
+ s(unigram_surprisal_prev2, bs="cr", k=6)
+ s(length, participant, bs="re")
+ s(length_prev, participant, bs="re")
+ s(length_prev2, participant, bs="re")
+ s(unigram_surprisal, participant,
bs="re")
+ s(unigram_surprisal_prev, participant,
bs="re")
+ s(unigram_surprisal_prev2, participant,
bs="re")
+ s(participant, bs="re")
\end{verbatim}
\end{minipage}\begin{minipage}[165.59853pt]\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Target model ($\varphi$).} The target model adds contextual surprisal and its two spillover lags to the baseline:
\begin{verbatim}
log(rt) ~ ... [baseline terms] ...
+ s(surprisal, bs="cr", k=6)
+ s(surprisal_prev, bs="cr", k=6)
+ s(surprisal_prev2, bs="cr", k=6)
+ s(surprisal, participant, bs="re")
+ s(surprisal_prev, participant, bs="re")
+ s(surprisal_prev2, participant, bs="re")
\end{verbatim}
\end{minipage}\vskip 10.22217pt\par\noindent Here {s(x, bs="cr", k=6)} denotes a cubic regression spline with 6 basis functions; {length} is unit length in characters (whitespace-inclusive: for the acontextual and model-tokens inventories, a unit's length includes any leading or trailing whitespace attributed to it by the transducer, so a mid-sentence word is one character longer than its raw spelling); {unigram\_surprisal} is unigram surprisal (see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:baseline}); {surprisal} is contextual surprisal from the language model; {\_prev} and {\_prev2} denote spillover from the first and second preceding unit, respectively. Each predictor enters as both a population-level smooth and a by-participant random slope {s(x, participant, bs="re")}; a random intercept for participants is included via {s(participant, bs="re")}.
For the character inventory, unit length is constant (every unit is a single character), so the {length}, {length\_prev}, and {length\_prev2} smooths are omitted from both the baseline and target formulas; the remaining terms and the random-effects structure are unchanged.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Paired permutation test.}
Significance of $\Delta_{\text{llh}}$ is assessed by a one-sided paired permutation test on the per-observation held-out log-likelihood differences between the target and baseline models, with $B=1000$ sign-flip permutations.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{Additional Results}
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:gamm_all} reports detailed GAMM results for each reading-time measure: mean per-observation held-out log-likelihood for the baseline ($\text{LL}_{\text{bl}}$) and target ($\text{LL}_{\text{tgt}}$) models, along with the improvement $\Delta_{\text{llh}}$ and 95\% trial-level bootstrap CIs. Note that absolute log-likelihoods are not comparable across unit inventories because the number and granularity of observations differ. \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:units_stats_gamm} reports the number of observations entering the regression for each inventory, after the additional exclusions specific to the GAMM input.

\@add@centering\end{table}\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:gamm_coefficients} shows the approximate F-statistics for all fixed-effect smooth terms in the full-data GAMM fit, broken down by predictor group and reading-time measure. We observe two patterns: First, the current-unit surprisal smooth is significant for every unit inventory and every reading-time measure. Second, length and spillover predictors contribute primarily to the word-like inventories (tokens, acontextual, contextual) and to the later measures (gaze duration and total reading time); at the character level, the length smooth is absent because all units share the same length.
